URL-encoded directory traversal (%2e%2e) against node 'st' static-file route (CVE-2014-3744) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts inbound web requests whose URI contains URL-encoded dot-dot sequences (%2e%2e / %252e%252e) that return HTTP 200 from a static-file route such as /public/. This targets the exact bypass in the Snyk 'File Explorer' CTF: the vulnerable 'st' npm module (<0.2.5) strips literal ../ but resolves URL-encoded dots, so %2e%2e%2f leaks files above the served directory. Rationale: Anchored on the exact CVE-2014-3744 bypass strings the article and Snyk PoC document: the 'st' module strips literal '../' but resolves URL-encoded dots, so %2e%2e / %252e%252e in a request that returns 200 is a high-fidelity leak signal (not a generic '../' template). Cross-checked GitHub Advisory GHSA-69rr-wvh9-6c4q and NVD for the <0.2.5 affected range and the %2e%2e mechanic. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-69rr-wvh9-6c4q • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2014-3744 • https://snyk.io/blog/snyking-in-directory-traversal-vulnerability-exploit-in-the-st-package/
ATT&CKT1190 T1083
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized DAST scans in CI/CD (Burp Suite, OWASP ZAP) probing the app with encoded traversal payloads
  • Pen-test engagements against the same host generating bursts of %2e%2e requests
  • Vulnerability scanners (Nessus, Qualys) firing the CVE-2014-3744 plugin
  • Legitimate application query parameters that URL-encode dots for non-traversal reasons (rare, and typically not returning 200 on a file path)
  • Security researchers / bug-bounty testers hitting the /public/ route
Suppression filter
| where CIp !in ('10.0.0.0','<known-scanner-ips>')  // replace with your DAST/scanner egress IPs
| where UserAgent !has "Nessus" and UserAgent !has "Qualys" and UserAgent !has "Burp" and UserAgent !has "ZAP"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full decoded URI and the ScStatus — a 200 means a file above the static root was served; identify the source IP, user-agent, and which file path resolved (look for /etc/passwd, .env, config, id_rsa, package.json). 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF/proxy, and confirm the app's 'st' package version — if <0.2.5, upgrade to >=0.2.5 (which replaces any '/../' urlencoded or not with '/') and redeploy. 3) Hunt: sweep the same source IP for other encoded-traversal attempts and successful 200s, review whether credential/secret files were returned, and rotate any secrets exposed in leaked files.
// Node 'st' static-server (CVE-2014-3744) URL-encoded traversal that actually returned data (HTTP 200)
// Primary source: Azure App Service HTTP logs. Swap to W3CIISLog (CsUriStem/CsUriQuery/scStatus) if the app is on IIS.
AppServiceHTTPLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| extend UriAll = strcat(tolower(CsUriStem), "?", tolower(CsUriQuery))
| where UriAll contains "%2e%2e" or UriAll contains "%252e%252e"   // url-encoded / double-encoded ../ — the exact 'st' bypass
| where ScStatus == 200                                            // 200 = file above the served root was actually leaked
| project TimeGenerated, CsHost, CIp, CsMethod, CsUriStem, CsUriQuery, ScStatus, UserAgent, Referer
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: URL-encoded directory traversal against node 'st' static-file server (CVE-2014-3744)
id: 6f3a9d2e-1c47-4b8a-9e21-5f0c8a7b3d14
status: experimental
description: Detects inbound web requests containing URL-encoded dot-dot sequences that bypass the literal ../ filter in the vulnerable 'st' npm module (<0.2.5), returning HTTP 200.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/fetch-the-flag-ctf-2022-writeup-file-explorer/
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-69rr-wvh9-6c4q
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2014-3744
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/17
tags:
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.t1083
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection_encoded:
    cs-uri-query|contains:
      - '%2e%2e'
      - '%252e%252e'
    cs-uri-stem|contains:
      - '%2e%2e'
      - '%252e%252e'
  selection_status:
    sc-status: 200
  condition: (1 of selection_encoded* ) and selection_status
falsepositives:
  - Authorized DAST / web vulnerability scanners (Burp, Nessus, Qualys)
  - Legitimate query parameters that carry percent-encoded literals
level: medium
CLOUD
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true allow_old_summaries=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Web where (Web.url="*%2e%2e*" OR Web.uri_path="*%2e%2e*" OR Web.url="*%252e%252e*" OR Web.url="*%2e%2e%2f*" OR Web.url="*%2e%2e%5c*") Web.status=200 by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.site, Web.http_method, Web.url, Web.uri_path, Web.status, Web.http_user_agent | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)` | sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false allow_old_summaries=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Web where (Web.url="*%2e%2e*" OR Web.uri_path="*%2e%2e*" OR Web.url="*%252e%252e*" OR Web.url="*%2e%2e%2f*" OR Web.url="*%2e%2e%5c*") Web.status=200 by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.site, Web.http_method, Web.url, Web.uri_path, Web.status, Web.http_user_agent | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)` | sort - count
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2014-3744")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2014-3744")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

NPM security: preventing supply chain attacks

2022-11-08 8 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

In this post, I intend to unveil npm security practices and tooling available for you as a JavaScript developer (TypeScript developers are welcome too).

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Lockfile injection: npm/yarn fetching dependencies from GitHub gist/repo instead of the registry
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] npm/yarn install-script (postinstall) spawning download LOLBins for secret theft / payload fetch · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — NPM security: preventing supply chain attacks
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] node-ipc destructive wiper component ssl-geospec.js written under node_modules\node-ipc\dao
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] node-ipc/peacenotwar protestware drops WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt on Desktop/OneDrive
node-ipc/peacenotwar protestware drops WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt on Desktop/OneDrive AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the signature file artifact written by the peacenotwar module that the article calls out (bundled into node-ipc v11/v9.2.2 and pulled in transitively by installs such as `npm install @vue/cli`). The module writes WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt in five languages to the user's Desktop and OneDrive folders during an npm install — a deterministic, high-fidelity indicator of a poisoned dependency tree. Rationale: Anchored on the exact filename WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt and Desktop/OneDrive drop location confirmed by BleepingComputer, Wikipedia and the GHSA advisory for the peacenotwar/node-ipc incident the article names; the string is unique enough to alert with near-zero FP. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-97m3-w2cp-4xx6 • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/big-sabotage-famous-npm-package-deletes-files-to-protest-ukraine-war/ • https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacenotwar • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-NODEIPC-2426370
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1491.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A security researcher intentionally detonating node-ipc in a sandbox/lab for analysis
  • A developer restoring a backup that already contained the dropped file
  • Threat-intel/test corpus files deliberately named WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt for detection validation
  • EDR test/purple-team exercises seeding the artifact
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the writing process is node.exe/npm/yarn and identify the offending package by inspecting node_modules and package-lock.json/yarn.lock for node-ipc, peacenotwar, or coloured-string deps; check the host's external IP geolocation (RU/BY hosts also risk the wiper). 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the install process, and quarantine the project's node_modules; block outbound to ipgeolocation APIs. 3) Hunt: sweep all dev/CI hosts for the same filename and for ssl-geospec.js under \node-ipc\dao\, and pin node-ipc to a safe version (>=10.1.3 / 9.2.2-removed builds) in lockfiles.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\Desktop\", @"\OneDrive")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "WITH-LOVE-FROM-AMERICA.txt"
| where TargetFilePath has_any (@"\Desktop\", @"\OneDrive")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

node-ipc destructive wiper component ssl-geospec.js written under node_modules\node-ipc\dao AIInstallation High
Hunts the malicious ssl-geospec.js dao module shipped in node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 (CVE-2022-23812) that geo-fences on RU/BY hosts then recursively overwrites files with a heart emoji. Detecting the file write under a node-ipc path catches the poisoned versions before or shortly after the destructive payload executes. Rationale: ssl-geospec.js in the node-ipc dao folder is the named malicious component (per Orca, BleepingComputer and the GHSA advisory) present only in the sabotaged 10.1.1/10.1.2 builds; the path+filename combination is a deterministic poisoned-version indicator. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-97m3-w2cp-4xx6 • https://orca.security/resources/blog/cve-2022-23812-protestware-malicious-code-node-ipc-npm-package/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/big-sabotage-famous-npm-package-deletes-files-to-protest-ukraine-war/
ATT&CKT1485 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Malware-analysis/lab detonation of the affected node-ipc version
  • Cached node-ipc tarballs restored from an old offline npm mirror
  • Purple-team replay of CVE-2022-23812 for detection validation
  • Security vendor sample repositories cloned to a dev box
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm node-ipc version via the package's package.json and lockfile entries; check whether the host's external IP geolocates to RU/BY (the wiper's trigger condition). 2) Contain: isolate the host immediately and halt any running node process before mass overwrite completes; preserve a disk image for recovery since the payload destroys file contents. 3) Hunt: scan all build agents and developer endpoints for node-ipc 10.1.1/10.1.2 in lockfiles, force upgrade to 10.1.3+, and review file-integrity logs for bulk overwrite activity by node.exe.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "ssl-geospec.js"
| where FolderPath has @"\node-ipc\"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "ssl-geospec.js"
| where TargetFilePath has @"\node-ipc\"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Lockfile injection: npm/yarn fetching dependencies from GitHub gist/repo instead of the registry AIDelivery Medium
Hunts the lockfile-injection technique the article details — an attacker edits the `resolved` URL in package-lock.json/yarn.lock to point at attacker-controlled GitHub gist/repo sources. On install, npm/yarn then downloads package tarballs from codeload.github.com / gist.githubusercontent.com / raw.githubusercontent.com rather than registry.npmjs.org, a network shape that betrays a tampered lockfile. Rationale: Directly models the article's lockfile-injection mechanism (attacker-controlled `resolved` key pointing at GitHub gist/repo) by flagging npm/yarn pulling tarballs from GitHub download hosts rather than registry.npmjs.org — the observable network side-effect of a poisoned lockfile. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/npm-security-preventing-supply-chain-attacks/
ATT&CKT1195.001 T1105
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate dependencies intentionally pinned to a GitHub repo/branch (common for forks and pre-release packages)
  • Developers running `npm install git+https://...` for internal tooling
  • CI fetching private GitHub-hosted packages by design
  • git-based monorepo workflows that resolve subpackages from GitHub
Suppression filter
// Defender: suppress approved internal GitHub orgs once enumerated
| where RemoteUrl !has "raw.githubusercontent.com/your-approved-org/"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the project's package-lock.json/yarn.lock and inspect the `resolved` field for the flagged dependency — confirm whether the GitHub source is an approved pin or an unexpected gist/repo, and review recent PRs touching the lockfile. 2) Contain: block the merge/build, revoke any CI secrets/API keys that the install job had access to, and remove the rogue resolved entry. 3) Hunt: run lockfile-lint across all repos to enforce a registry-only trust policy, and review CI logs for the same GitHub download hosts across other pipelines.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","yarn.exe")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","yarn add")
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("codeload.github.com","gist.githubusercontent.com","raw.githubusercontent.com")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstHostname has_any ("codeload.github.com","gist.githubusercontent.com","raw.githubusercontent.com")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcHostname, SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: c4e8d913-7a26-4f55-9b0e-3a8f2d6c1b77

  category: dns
  product: windows
detection:

    QueryName:
      - 'codeload.github.com'
      - 'gist.githubusercontent.com'
      - 'raw.githubusercontent.com'

level: medium
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:3 @Image:(*\\node.exe OR *\\node.EXE OR *\\npm.exe OR *\\yarn.exe) @DestinationHostname:(codeload.github.com OR gist.githubusercontent.com OR raw.githubusercontent.com)
#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/(codeload\.github\.com|gist\.githubusercontent\.com|raw\.githubusercontent\.com)/i

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Network_Resolution where DNS.query IN ("codeload.github.com","gist.githubusercontent.com","raw.githubusercontent.com") by DNS.src DNS.query | `drop_dm_object_name(DNS)` | sort - lastTime
npm/yarn install-script (postinstall) spawning download LOLBins for secret theft / payload fetch AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the arbitrary-command-execution risk the article flags — npm/yarn install hooks (pre/postinstall) running on a developer or CI host can execute attacker commands. This targets node/npm/yarn spawning download-capable LOLBins (powershell, curl, certutil, bitsadmin) with a URL or web-client string in the command line, the shape used to exfiltrate CI secrets or pull a second-stage payload during install. Rationale: Models the article's explicit 'npm install may execute arbitrary commands' warning by pairing the node/npm/yarn parent with a download-LOLBin child plus a URL/web-client cmdline token — the combination (not the LOLBin alone) is what indicates a malicious install hook stealing CI secrets or fetching a payload. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/npm-security-preventing-supply-chain-attacks/
ATT&CKT1059 T1195.002 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • node-gyp / native-module builds invoking cmd/powershell during legitimate installs
  • Packages that legitimately download platform binaries on postinstall (e.g. puppeteer, electron, esbuild) via curl/Invoke-WebRequest
  • CI bootstrap scripts that fetch tooling over HTTPS during `npm ci`
  • Developer scripts wrapping npm with PowerShell automation
Suppression filter
// Defender: suppress known-good binary-downloading installers once baselined
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("puppeteer","electron","esbuild","node-gyp","playwright"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full child command line and destination URL, identify the package whose pre/postinstall script spawned it (inspect package.json scripts in the active project), and reputation-check the URL/host. 2) Contain: kill the process chain, isolate the host if the URL is unknown/malicious, and rotate any CI secrets/tokens the install job could read. 3) Hunt: pivot on the destination host across DeviceNetworkEvents, sweep other repos for the same package/version, and add the package to a deny policy until reviewed.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","yarn.exe")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm install","npm ci","yarn install","postinstall","preinstall")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","certutil.exe","bitsadmin.exe","wget.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("http://","https://","DownloadString","Invoke-WebRequest","-urlcache","Net.WebClient","iwr ")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node.exe","npm.exe","yarn.exe")
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","certutil.exe","bitsadmin.exe","wget.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe")
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("http://","https://","DownloadString","Invoke-WebRequest","-urlcache","Net.WebClient")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine,
          TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: npm/yarn Install Hook Spawning Download LOLBin
id: 8f1d6b40-2e57-4c98-a1f3-6b9c2e4d7f10
description: Detects node/npm/yarn spawning a download-capable LOLBin with a URL or web-client string, indicative of a malicious install script
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/npm-security-preventing-supply-chain-attacks/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/27
tags:
  - attack.t1059
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1105
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\npm.exe'
      - '\yarn.exe'
  selection_child:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\curl.exe'
      - '\certutil.exe'
      - '\bitsadmin.exe'
      - '\wget.exe'
      - '\mshta.exe'
  selection_net:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'http://'
      - 'https://'
      - 'DownloadString'
      - 'Invoke-WebRequest'
      - '-urlcache'
      - 'Net.WebClient'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_net
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\node.exe OR *\\npm.exe OR *\\yarn.exe) @Image:(*\\powershell.exe OR *\\PowerShell.exe OR *\\cmd.exe OR *\\curl.exe OR *\\certutil.exe OR *\\bitsadmin.exe OR *\\mshta.exe) @CommandLine:(*http* OR *DownloadString* OR *Invoke-WebRequest* OR *Net.WebClient* OR *-urlcache*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/(node|npm|yarn)\.exe$/i
| ImageFileName=/(powershell|pwsh|cmd|curl|certutil|bitsadmin|wget|mshta|wscript|cscript)\.exe$/i
| regex("(?i)(https?://|DownloadString|Invoke-WebRequest|Net\\.WebClient|-urlcache)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node.exe","npm.exe","yarn.exe")) AND (Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","certutil.exe","bitsadmin.exe","wget.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe")) AND (Processes.process="*http*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-WebRequest*" OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*" OR Processes.process="*-urlcache*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process Processes.process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.parent_process_name IN ("node.exe","npm.exe","yarn.exe")) AND (Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","curl.exe","certutil.exe","bitsadmin.exe","wget.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe")) AND (Processes.process="*http*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-WebRequest*" OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*" OR Processes.process="*-urlcache*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process Processes.process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | sort - lastTime
Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-23812")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-23812")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities

ATT&CKT1195.002

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT

Microsoft Windows Mark of the Web (MOTW) contains a security feature bypass vulnerability resulting in a limited loss of integrity and availability of security features. Vendor: Microsoft, Product: Windows. Known ransomware use: Known. Federal patch due: 2022-12-09.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-41091")

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,

// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-41091")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium

ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc

ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'

falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.

| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2022-41125")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High

// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["56ca24b57c4559f834c190d50b0fe89dd4a4040a078ca1f267d0bbc7849e9ed7", "af5fb99d3ff18bc625fb63f792ed7cd955171ab509c2f8e7c7ee44515e09cebf", "926a947ea2b59d3e9a5a6875b4de2bd071b15260370f4da5e2a60ece3517a32f", "3bff571823421c013e79cc10793f238f4252f7d7ac91f9ef41435af0a8c09a39", "c49b4d370ad0dcd1e28ee8f525ac8e3c12a34cfcf62ebb733ec74cca59b29f82", "08f93351d0d3905bee5b0c2b9215d448abb0d3cf49c0f8b666c46df4fcc007cb"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: File or Process Matching Article Hash IOCs
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0b
status: experimental
description: |

CISA KEV: CVE-2021-25337 — Samsung Mobile Devices Improper Access Control Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2022-11-08 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Samsung mobile devices using Mali GPU contain an incorrect implementation handling file descriptor in dpu driver. This incorrect implementation results in memory corruption, leading to kernel panic. This vulnerability was chained with CVE-2021-25337 and CVE-2021-25369. Vendor: Samsung, Product: Mobile Devices. Federal patch due: 2022-11-29.

CVEsCVE-2021-25370 CVE-2021-25337 CVE-2021-25369

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)

Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-25370", "CVE-2021-25337", "CVE-2021-25369")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-25370", "CVE-2021-25337", "CVE-2021-25369")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Key points from Google and Accenture's ransomware white paper

Snyk
2022-11-07 9 use cases 7 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

Author's from Accenture and Google Cloud recently released a white paper, which details the current state of ransomware and solutions to address this growing problem. This article will provide an overview of the key points from the white paper.

ATT&CKT1021.001 T1190 T1195.002 T1204.002 T1486 T1566 T1566.001
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
Detected
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    from datamodel=Email.All_Email

| rename All_Email.recipient as user
| join type=inner user
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
        from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

        by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
     | rename Processes.user as user]

title: Office Application Spawning Script Host
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d07
status: experimental
description: |
    Word/Excel/Outlook/OneNote spawning powershell, cmd, wscript,
    cscript, mshta, rundll32, regsvr32 — classic macro / embedded-
    object payload signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059
    - attack.t1566.001
    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\winword.exe'
            - '\excel.exe'
            - '\powerpnt.exe'
            - '\outlook.exe'
            - '\onenote.exe'
            - '\mspub.exe'
            - '\visio.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high

| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)` | `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`

ATT&CKT1204.004 T1059.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents

| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:

author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02

    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access

    category: process_access
    product: windows

        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high

        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Medium

A day in the life of a CISO: Chris Hughes of Aquia

Snyk
2022-11-04 1 use case 2 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Find out what a day in the life of a CISO and co-founder is like with this discussion between Chris Hughes (CIS & Co-Founder) of Aquia and Vandana Verma of Snyk.

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
—
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Likely
Phase 6
Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Critical

Secure Python URL validation

Snyk
2022-11-04 7 use cases 3 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

In this article, we’ll explore the challenges of corrupted URLs, how they can damage your applications, and ways to tackle the problem.

ATT&CKT1190 T1486 T1566

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Secure Python URL validation
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

2022-11-03 2 use cases 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

OpenSSL has released two high severity vulnerabilities — CVE-2022-3602 and CVE-2022-3786 — related to buffer overrun.

CVEsCVE-2022-3602 CVE-2022-3786
ATT&CKT1190

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
